<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>The audit is running in the background (37 agents, three phases). What it does:</p>
<p><strong>Phase 1 — Extract (31 sonnet agents, one per chapter).</strong> Prompt tuned on the three probes, plus two additions the probes showed were needed: a <strong>Back-references</strong> section (things a chapter treats as already true but doesn't establish — the cross-chapter joints), and an <strong>Author's notes / specialist terms</strong> section to reconcile against the farming glossary. Each writes to <code>notes/audit/chapters/NNN-slug.md</code>. They're deliberately barred from reading canon, so their record of what's on the page is independent of what canon claims is on the page.</p>
<p><strong>Phase 2 — Reconcile (5 opus agents).</strong> Partitioned by domain with <strong>disjoint canon file ownership</strong> so they can edit in parallel without colliding:</p>
<div class="table-wrap"><table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Domain</th>
<th>Owns</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>system-and-magic</td>
<td><code>system.md</code>, <code>magic.md</code>, <code>dungeons.md</code>, <code>healing.md</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>characters</td>
<td><code>characters/**</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>world</td>
<td>the rest of <code>world/</code> incl. glossary and institutions</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>timeline</td>
<td><code>timeline.md</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>theme-and-narrative</td>
<td>nothing — read-and-report only</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<p>Each does completeness (missing facts into canon at the right claim level and citation), consistency (chapter-vs-canon, chapter-vs-chapter, canon-vs-canon, with the arithmetic actually done), answers to the accumulated questions, and flags load-bearing mysteries with no written answer. They're bound by your standing rules: never close an Open question, never weaken canon to fit a draft, sealed material described by shape only.</p>
<p>**Phase 3 — Integrate (1 opus agent). Cross-checks the five reports against each other, independently verifies the load-bearing contradictions before they make the final list, audits the domain agents' own canon edits for overreach (an <code>[implied]</code> fact promoted to Established, an Open question quietly closed), and writes <code>notes/audit/REPORT.md</code>.</p>
<p>The probes already turned up two candidate findings for it to chase: ch. 18's delve total of 406 kills doesn't reconcile with its itemized chamber counts, and Edwin and <span class="redacted">redacted</span>'s 80ft/70ft depth estimates go unresolved.</p>
<p>Two notes on choices I made. I had the extractors write to files rather than return prose, so the synthesis stage reads from disk instead of everything routing through one context. And I put the audit output under <code>notes/audit/</code> in the repo rather than scratchpad — it's a durable record you'll likely want to diff against on the next pass, though say the word and I'll move it. I'll report when it lands.</p>
</div>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> some drills like that seem useful, but a day every week is the wrong structure and I don't think it scratches the surface of the real problem. it is ironically a very shallow answer.</p>
<p>I think the real things you need to do are:</p>
<ol>
<li>evaluate the actual outcomes of your actions and look for ways to improve. sometimes you can use system feedback on this (a crafting skill includes evaluating quality) sometimes you can't (there are things you might or should care about that the system doesn't, especially appropriateness to the situation)</li>
<li>be able to do things well in ways the system nudges you against. it always pushes you onto the standard path, so you need to step off it.</li>
<li>understand why doing things the right way is the right way. try other ways and see if they were wrong. come up with explanations.</li>
<li>observe the world and get ahead of the nudge. don't rely on the system for pointing out details you should be able to spot yourself.</li>
<li>acquire your own set of taste and preferences about how to do things.</li>
<li>you should be able to do tasks perfectly in the system way without ever triggering the nudge (which only happens when you do something the system thinks is wrong, or about to). know the rules to break the rules.</li>
</ol>
<p>probably many others. there's no one single trick to getting good at things. draw on expertise studies (deliberate practice, naturalised decision making, etc.) and write up some detailed notes on this</p></blockquote>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p><code>notes/getting-good.md</code>, with the drill day demoted in <code>system.md</code> and <code>peri.md</code> rather than struck.</p>
<p>The most useful thing I found is that one of your six points has an exact, well-replicated result behind it. The <strong>guidance hypothesis</strong> in motor learning (Salmoni, Schmidt &amp; Walter): frequent concurrent feedback <em>improves performance while present and degrades learning</em> — learners given constant augmented feedback look excellent in practice and fail on retention and transfer when it's withdrawn, because they tune to the guidance instead of the movement. That is the nudge, precisely, and it means the System's failure mode isn't laziness; it's dependence that is invisible during practice because during practice the work is genuinely good. Paired with <strong>arrested development</strong> (Fitts &amp; Posner's autonomous stage stops improving), the stall stops being a mechanic canon asserts and becomes a real thing with a real cause.</p>
<p>The structural fix has a name too: <strong>deliberate performance</strong> (Fadde &amp; Klein) is the literature's own answer to &quot;professionals can't stop working to practise&quot; — embed it in the job, estimate before you measure, commit before you're told. So &quot;a day a week is the wrong unit&quot; isn't a preference, and I didn't have to invent the alternative.</p>
<p>Two things I'd flag from writing it.</p>
<p><strong>The fifty guesses is already better than the thing built to echo it.</strong> It's unscheduled, done while walking somewhere else, and the guess precedes the answer — that's the generation effect and deliberate performance, correctly built, in chapter 008. The drill day is the weaker sibling of your best teaching scene. I kept the drill day rather than cutting it because <span class="redacted">redacted</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> It just isn't a defence of the method.</p>
<p><strong>Discipline 3 — understanding why the right way is right, by doing it wrong — is the same instinct as a control.</strong> Edwin's science and Edwin's pedagogy are one thing, and neither he nor the narration has ever said so. That's the cheapest replacement available: no separate time, already the book's texture.</p>
<p>Also worth your attention: several of these Peri is already doing unprompted — the slate over the census figures at <span class="redacted">redacted</span> is disciplines 1 and 3 arriving together — and canon files her house style under &quot;Method, grown&quot;, which is the right instinct under a slightly wrong name. It's taste, and taste diverging from his is what succession looks like from inside.</p>
<p>The section I'd most want you to read is the last one, on how this is allowed on the page: no character ever holds the list, no coinages, no research vocabulary, and the method must sometimes be unnecessary. A named System of Getting Good is the shallow answer wearing a longer coat. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence</span></p>
</div>

If the coverage map only tracks what the skill itself teaches, then everything outside the skill never shows up as a gap because it was never in scope — so needing nothing from the System is compatible with being merely workmanlike. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> It doesn't buy good. <blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>I think probably a practice you do for some amount of time every day works better than one you do one day a week?</p></blockquote>


<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>That's better than what I wrote, and it also rescues the argument I'd just given up.</p>
<p>Three things support it. <strong>Spacing</strong>: same total time, more sessions, better retention — daily beats weekly-blocked straightforwardly. <strong>Overnight consolidation</strong>: motor skills consolidate during sleep, so daily practice gets a consolidation episode per session where weekly gets one per week. And <strong>habit formation</strong> (Lally and others on context-dependent repetition): a behaviour becomes automatic when it repeats against a stable recurring cue. A daily cue — after supper, before the horses — recurs often enough to build that. A weekly one never does.</p>
<p>Which means weekly is the worst of both worlds: too rare to become a habit, too blocked to be well spaced. And it inverts <span class="redacted">redacted</span>'s objection. A daily practice attached to a fixed daily cue is <em>more</em> robust to convenience than a weekly appointment, not less — you can't skip Thursday if there is no Thursday. So the tension I left standing actually resolves in your favour.</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Daily gives you twenty points in three weeks. Daily practice makes the scoring argument <em>stronger</em>, not weaker.</p>
</div>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Workflow's running in the background (10 agents, ~4 phases). I extracted the four epubs to plain text first so the study agents can grep and sample rather than choke on them — Herriot alone is 461k words.</p>
<p>Shape of it:</p>
<p><strong>Study</strong> (parallel) — one agent per book, each briefed on what <em>we</em> need rather than general craft. Herriot: how routine professional work becomes gripping, and farmers as individuals. McPhee: how to make earth science thrilling with no plot and no peril — his use of numbers especially, and building dread out of slow process. Weir: the anatomy of a problem-solving sequence and how much arithmetic you can show. Pratchett: the Chalk material specifically, not the Feegles — worked land, inherited practical knowledge, Granny Aching built out of other people's concrete detail. Alongside them, an opus agent diagnosing the <em>current</em> voice from the prose first and the notes second, with actual measurement (sentence-length distribution, polysyndeton frequency, how often a sentence carries a number). It's briefed to test a specific hypothesis and to argue with it: that the prose reports discovery at the same temperature as it reports breakfast, and that the narrator's refusal to acknowledge an open question is what removes suspense. It's also asked which of our own binding rules are <em>causing</em> the flatness — the endings-are-cuts rule is the obvious suspect.</p>
<p><strong>Synthesize</strong> — one opus agent writes three candidate voices, with a constraint I think matters: they must be three different answers to &quot;what makes a reader turn the page&quot;, not three shades of one, and none of them may just be &quot;the Herriot one&quot;. Each keeps the current voice as base.</p>
<p><strong>Write</strong> — one agent per voice doing all three scenes, so the voice stays consistent across them. Scenes chosen as three different tests: <strong>013</strong>'s opening (the barley ripening in bands — the mystery test), <strong>010</strong>'s chaining (procedure, the McPhee/Weir test), <strong>018</strong>'s delve opening (pace and threat, and the one place the book has conventional adventure material).</p>
<p><strong>Judge</strong> — reads all nine against the source chapters, picks a winner per scene, and proposes concrete amendments to <code>voices.md</code>/<code>llm-tells.md</code> without editing them, since those are your call.</p>
<p>Everything lands in <code>samples/round5/</code>. The final agent's report is written for reading on a phone — it'll quote one line per voice so you can hear the difference without opening anything.</p>
</div>

<p>Done — 10 agents, all nine samples written, everything committed to <code>samples/round5/</code>.</p>
<h2>The diagnosis first, because it's better than I expected</h2>
<p>The agent measured all 31 chapters rather than opining, and the finding is sharp:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>The narration has adopted the epistemology of its own characters.</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p>Edwin's discipline — never state more than the evidence supports, never grade a result, write it down and wait two months — has migrated out of the dialogue and into the narrative frame. Excellent scientist, catastrophic storyteller, because suspense is made entirely of things the narration is willing to assert and then not resolve.</p>
<p>Two numbers carry it:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>46% of your 165 scenes open with a date- or clock-stamp</strong>, and another 10% open by announcing how long the scene will take. &quot;<span class="redacted">redacted</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> The reader is reading the day book, and the day book is what you consult when you already know how it came out.</li>
<li><strong>Zero question marks in narration across 23,000 words.</strong> Every question in the book is inside quotation marks and nearly all are answered within three lines.</li>
</ul>
<p>And the trend line, which is the alarming part: from ch. 1 to ch. 30 mean sentence length nearly doubles, sentences containing a number triple (17% → 57%), polysyndeton quadruples, dialogue share halves, figures of speech thin to a third. <strong>The book has been steadily becoming its own appendix.</strong> Ch. 1 doesn't read like this at all — it has narratorial opinion, comic timing, 40% dialogue.</p>
<p>It also flags that ch. 13's real finding — four circles whose <em>centres are walking north-east across the country</em> — is converted into a methodological caution about arithmetic within one line, then cut away from.</p>
<h2>The three voices</h2>
<p><strong>The Rising Water</strong> — McPhee. The reader turns the page because they can picture the world in which this thing wins. Translated numbers, unglossed symptom paragraphs, dread by accumulation, consequence always attached to a named acre.</p>
<p><strong>The Open Wager</strong> — mystery as competing hypotheses the reader can score. Two named live theories at all times, arithmetic done one step ahead of Edwin, a wrong theory sustained for a whole arc, plus a colder documentary register that lets the reader know what he doesn't.</p>
<p><strong>The Call-Out</strong> — Herriot. A job is going wrong in someone's yard while you watch. Comedy running <em>under</em> unresolved jeopardy, technical content only as defence or dispute, the mystery advancing as a by-product of work that can't be put off.</p>
<p>Same paragraph, three ways:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Rising Water:</strong> &quot;That is the whole list. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
</blockquote>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Open Wager:</strong> &quot;That is the list. There is nothing else on it. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Four of them. Curved.&quot;</p>
</blockquote>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<h2>The verdict</h2>
<p>The Call-Out for the sentences, the Open Wager for the architecture, one instrument from the Rising Water.</p>
<p>The Call-Out won two scenes of three — and won the two the book is mostly made of, ordinary procedure and <span class="redacted">redacted</span>. Its fuel is the only renewable one: dread depletes, live hypotheses deplete and this mystery has few, but a named person wanting something done with money on it is inexhaustible, and the story is already two people going farm to farm at other people's request. It's also the smallest departure from what you have.</p>
<p>It <em>failed</em> the discovery scene, exactly as its own spec predicted — the yard got so interesting the four circles never made it into the scene. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Those belong in arc plans. Twice in its own samples the Open Wager had to write a paragraph whose only job was to keep score, and both times broke its own bans to do it. The scoreboard can't be carried by the narrator.</p>
<p><strong>The headline amendment: openings are entrances, not minutes.</strong> No scene may open on a sentence stating its own outcome, duration, or verdict. All three voices invented this rule independently, all nine samples have a better first sentence than their source, and it's the mirror image of endings-are-cuts — which is what pushed all the weight to the front in the first place. Runner-up: the epistemic downgrade rationed to one finding in four. The move is sacred; the rate is fatal.</p>
<p>The lab's own stated limit is scale — nine rewrites of 1,100 words each, all by the same hand that wrote the specs, and every failure that matters is a <em>rate</em> failure. Its proposed next test is one full 4,000-word chapter in the fusion, run through the same census. Say the word and I'll run it.</p>
</div>

<p><strong>Spec</strong> — an opus agent turns the report's verdict into a usable instrument rather than a summary. <p><strong>Census</strong> — sonnet, mechanical: reproduces the round-5 methodology exactly, runs on the fusion chapter plus ch. 13, ch. 10 and ch. 1 as reference points. <p><strong>Judge</strong> — reads the chapter cold and writes down the reading experience <em>before</em> looking at the numbers. The two lists that matter are metrics inside target where the prose is nonetheless worse, and metrics outside target that don't matter; where the reading and the census disagree is the most informative thing this test can produce. It rules on the writer's objections one by one and commits to adopt / adopt-with-amendments / don't, with exact replacement wording — as a proposal, not an edit, since <code>voices.md</code> is yours.</p>
</div>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Running. Here's what the probe established and how I built around it.</p>
<p><strong>Technically viable, with one constraint.</strong> Subagents get <code>SendMessage</code> but not <code>ListAgents</code> — so they can't discover their siblings. The error message is the useful part: it says to use the agent ID from a spawn result. So an agent <em>can</em> message agents <strong>it spawned itself</strong>, and those keep their context between messages. That means the director has to be an agent that spawns its own cast, rather than me spawning a flat pool and introducing them. I've told it to verify messaging on one agent before spawning the other five, and to report a negative result rather than quietly writing the scene alone — a failure here is a real finding and I don't want it papered over.</p>
<p><strong>The cast:</strong> a Director (composes, writes no prose of its own), a Narrator (narration only, never a line of dialogue), and one agent each for Edwin, <span class="redacted">redacted</span>, <span class="redacted">redacted</span>, <span class="redacted">redacted</span> and <span class="redacted">redacted</span>. Each character agent reads only its own character file, its own section of <code>dialogue-voices.md</code>, and the dialogue rules — and is explicitly told <strong>not to read the source chapter and not to read the other characters' files.</strong> They must not know what the others sound like. That isolation is the whole experiment.</p>
<p><strong>The protocol is the actual variable,</strong> so I made it strict. When the director asks for a line it may send only: the situation in its own words, what the character <em>wants</em> from the line (your existing dialogue rule 1), and at most <strong>one</strong> immediately preceding line quoted verbatim, labelled as what was just said to them. It may not send the surrounding narration, the draft so far, other lines, or any description of how someone else talks. If it catches itself pasting prose &quot;for the feel&quot;, that's the mirroring it's testing for. It asks for two or three alternatives each time and rejects in terms of <em>want</em>, never style — and it may not rewrite what an agent gives it. Rejected lines go back to their owner.</p>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> the opening of ch. 019, the four dead names struck off the roster. Five speakers at one table plus a silent Peri — the hardest possible case for register drift, and one where the source is a fair baseline.</p>
<p><strong>Measurement:</strong> the report has to score both the source scene and the new one on your own two instruments from <code>dialogue-voices.md</code> — prop-cover and the swap test. Numbers, not impressions. That matters because your own closing assessment found the swap test scoring 3/6 and 2/6 where attribution scored 24/28: if isolation is doing anything real, it should show up in the swap, not the attribution.</p>
<p>It's also briefed to hunt the predicted failure specifically — lines that are perfectly in character but wrong for the moment, because the speaker couldn't see the room. That's the thing this architecture should cost you, and I'd rather have it named than discovered in chapter 40.</p>
<p>The round-5 fusion workflow is still running independently; I've fenced the two off each other's files.</p>
</div>

<p>The correction: character agents now get the scene in full — narration verbatim, every line spoken so far attributed, plus what their character knows, wants and is worried about at that point. A person at that table hears everything, and withholding it doesn't make them more themselves, it makes them vague.</p>
<p>What I got wrong was locating the mechanism. I'd assumed isolation meant information asymmetry, when the thing actually worth testing is that the context generating <span class="redacted">redacted</span>'s line has spent its whole existence <em>being</em> <span class="redacted">redacted</span>, rather than being one narrator ventriloquising five people and optimising each line for local coherence with the last. Persona-primed generation, not starvation.</p>
<p>So the surviving constraints are narrower and, I think, the right ones: one agent per character, each writing only their own lines; no character agent ever reads another's <strong>character file or voice spec</strong> — they can hear what someone says, since that's what being in a room is, but they're never handed a specification to match; they don't read the source chapter, so the scene is arrived at rather than recalled; and they stay in role throughout, accumulating the scene as a participant. The per-line <em>want</em> stays, since that was doing real work.</p>
<p>I also told it to keep any lines it had already produced under the starved protocol and put a side-by-side of a starved line against its fully-informed replacement in the findings. If it got far enough to have some, that comparison is now probably the most informative thing the experiment yields.</p>

On voice alone the method still goes 7% → 36%, which is a real fivefold gain, but it's a fifth the size of the headline.</p>
<p><strong>Its recommendation is against itself: don't do this for real.</strong> It judges that most of the gain came from writing each line's <em>want</em> as a role-bound instruction before generating it — not from context isolation. Its proposal is to keep the wants and drop the agents: write the dialogue as a list of speaker-plus-want slots before drafting, then draft normally, and reserve the full multi-agent method as a repair tool for a single character dissolving in the editing pass. <p>My inclination on fixing this is that we should actually ditch the chapter structure entirely in first writing, as I think it's ended up as one of the things that wrecks the voice, and we should write the book in a scene-based manner and then do a chapter-based editing pass afterwards.</p>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Agree with both, and the evidence in the repo is stronger than you're claiming.</p>
<p><strong>On length, you're not proposing a change so much as enforcement.</strong> <code>notes/royal-road-style.md</code> already cites the RR consensus at 1,500–3,000 and says in terms that &quot;several 2–2.5k chapters beat one long one&quot; — then sets the project target at 2,500–3,500, at the top of that range. Actual practice is 3.0–4.8k, mean 3.6k, with every single chapter over 3k. The target isn't binding and hasn't been for thirty-one chapters. That same note flags padding as an <em>existential</em> risk at daily cadence, not a style nit. And the fusion chapter came out at 4,879 — the new voice drifted longer, not shorter.</p>
<p><strong>On scene-first, the mechanism is already measured.</strong> The chapter is currently both the unit of composition and the unit of publication, and that conflation is what the census caught: &quot;endings are cuts&quot; removed the cadence from the end, and the weight went to the front — 46% of scenes now open date-stamped, 10% more announce their own duration. A scene inside a chapter doesn't need to announce itself. A chapter does, because it has to orient a reader arriving a day later. Stop drafting in chapters and roughly half those minute-openings lose their reason to exist. That's the single biggest lever the diagnosis found, and this gets at it structurally rather than by adding another rule.</p>
<p><strong>The argument for it that neither of us has made yet:</strong> it makes cutting mid-scene possible. Right now chapters end where scenes end, because the chapter is what gets composed. Write continuous scene-flow and <span class="redacted">redacted</span> can fall <em>inside</em> a scene — which is the strongest serial hook there is, and is currently structurally impossible. Chaotic Craftsman, your own retention model, does exactly this.</p>
<p><strong>Where I'd push:</strong></p>
<p>Scene-first doesn't control length by itself; it relocates the unit. You need a scene target — 600–900 words, given 165 scenes across 113k — and the device budgets must become per-thousand-words rather than per-scene, or more scenes simply means more quota. That's precisely what inflated the fusion chapter: the verdict found its quotas &quot;add rather than trade,&quot; and independently proposed per-1,000-word spans. Same fix, arrived at from the other end.</p>
<p>And the chapter pass must group and cut, never rewrite. If it starts smoothing prose it becomes a second homogenising pass over every scene, which is the voice-collapse mechanism wearing a different hat.</p>
<p>Keep chapters in <em>planning</em> for pacing and schedule — arc-01 is already at roughly scene granularity, so this is a smaller change to the process docs than it sounds.</p>
<p>Want me to write it up as a proposal against <code>notes/process.md</code>?</p>
</div>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Written to <code>notes/scene-first-proposal.md</code> and committed — marked Working and unruled, with exact replacement wording for <code>process.md</code>, <code>CLAUDE.md</code>, and the three skills, so adopting it is a series of small edits.</p>
<p>Two things I found while writing it that change the shape of your proposal.</p>
<p><strong>The project has three different chapter-length targets, and the loosest one is the one the drafting skills quote.</strong> <code>royal-road-style.md</code> cites platform consensus at 1,500–3,000 and then sets 2,500–3,500; <code>process.md</code>, <code>story-management</code> and <code>write-arc</code> all say <strong>3–5k, hard cap 8k</strong>. A drafter reading the skill is told 4,800 words is comfortably normal. Actual output: mean 3,642, and <strong>27 of 31 chapters exceed the consensus ceiling</strong> its own research cites. So your instinct that they're too long isn't a new judgement — it's the platform research the project already did, which the drafting instructions then overrode.</p>
<p><strong>And the thing that would have bitten you: <code>voices.md</code> expresses about ten binding budgets per <em>chapter</em>.</strong> One windup dialogue tag per chapter, one type-of-person clause, one generalising clause, two one-word paragraphs, ~11 &quot;which&quot; clauses, and so on. Those were written against a 3,600-word chapter. Move to 2,000 and <strong>every one of them silently loosens by 45%</strong> — the tells they exist to suppress get denser, while every rule still reports as satisfied. That's the worst failure mode available, because it's invisible to the instruments.</p>
<p>So the recommendation is: adopt both halves, but land the per-1,000-word budget conversion <em>first or simultaneously</em>. That conversion is already drafted as amendment A2 of the round-5 verdict, arrived at from completely different evidence — distribution failures the per-chapter rates couldn't see. The same fix solves both problems, which is the best sign available that it's the right shape.</p>
<p>The argument I'd most want you to weigh is (b) in §3: scene-first makes <strong>cutting mid-scene possible</strong>, which is currently structurally impossible because chapters end where scenes end. <code>voices.md</code> already names Chaotic Craftsman as the model <em>because</em> it cuts mid-flow — so the project adopted that aesthetic and kept a production structure that forbids its strongest form.</p>
<p>And a falsifier, since this is a large change on a diagnosis: draft one arc scene-first, assemble it, and check whether the 46% minute-opening rate actually falls. If it doesn't, the mechanism in §2 is wrong and the restructure bought nothing — worth knowing before an arc is written the new way rather than after.</p>
</div>

All four defects in its scene were register bleed into Edwin's constructions — his grading shape, his copula-plus-possessive refusal. That's exactly what isolation prevents by construction. So they're complements, not substitutes, and &quot;which one do we keep&quot; was the wrong question. Provisional position: keep the wants at 1.2–1.4x a normal pass, and add a construction-ownership check to <code>editing-pass</code> for the failure they can't catch.</p>

<p><strong>Phase 1 — sealed answers</strong> (13 agents). A pipeline per mystery: draft an answer → three adversaries attack it in parallel on different lenses → revise and seal. The lenses are deliberately different rather than three copies of &quot;is this right&quot;: one re-checks the on-page evidence <em>without trusting the draft's own evidence table</em>, one attacks it on <code>theme.md</code> and <code>premise.md</code> (does it smuggle in a great evil, which anchor 2 forbids), and one attacks it as a story mechanism — is it dramatisable in a field by people doing work, or only ever tellable. Adversaries default to &quot;refuted&quot; when uncertain, because a weak answer that survives here gets clues planted against it.</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted</span>, since the existing sealed answer forbids exactly that. M2 also has to state <strong>what the Frostmonth tide measurement reads</strong> — that's the promise on the page that makes &quot;decide late&quot; untenable.</p>
<p>A final agent checks all four sealed answers against each other and looks for a <em>fifth</em> mystery now accumulating evidence.</p>
<p><strong>Phases 2a + 3 — decisions and consolidation</strong> (5 agents), on disjoint file ownership so they can't collide with each other or with Phase 1, which only touches <code>notes/sealed/</code>. Four domain agents each rule their decisions <em>and</em> triage their open questions in the same pass, then a sweep agent does cross-domain integration and the stale-status hunt.</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> All deferred triggers get collected into <code>notes/canon/deferred-questions.md</code>, <span class="unpub-tag">unpublished</span> sorted by how soon they fire.</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence</span></p>
<p>Still to come once these land: Phase 2b (the two dependent rulings), Phase 4 (rewrite plan, arc plans at scene granularity, and the clue ledger), then Phase 5's pilot arc and gate. I'll report as each returns.</p>
</div>

<p>Quota is back and <strong>Phase 5 is running</strong> — the last phase of the plan.</p>
<p>The pilot arc: ~42 scenes, ~32k words, drafted <strong>sequentially by movement</strong> rather than in parallel. That's deliberate and costs wall-clock: each movement agent reads everything written before it and is told it's continuing one continuous piece of prose, not writing an independent unit. Parallel drafting would have been faster and would have produced five subtly different books — which is the voice-collapse failure this whole restructure exists to prevent, arriving by a new route.</p>
<p>Then a line-editing pass in three overlapping ranges, running the <strong>construction-ownership check</strong> added earlier today — the failure a want-list cannot catch by construction. Editors are told explicitly to fix what's wrong and flag judgement calls, but <strong>not to revoice</strong>, since a second homogenising sweep over every scene would undo the point.</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<p>Then the gate. Three criteria fixed in advance:</p>
<div class="table-wrap"><table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Measure</th>
<th>Baseline</th>
<th>Pass</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Date/clock-stamped scene openings</td>
<td>46%</td>
<td>under 20%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Duration-announcing openings</td>
<td>10%</td>
<td>under 5%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<p>The gate agent reads the arc <strong>cold and writes that down before looking at any number</strong> — where the reading and the census disagree is the most informative thing the exercise can produce. It's told which four census rows are struck as unreliable and must not be scored, that the distribution block is the pass/fail row, and that comedy beats need hand-counting because no script can see them. The dialogue instruments run as per-line diagnostics only, never as totals, per this morning's blind re-score.</p>
<p>And the instruction that matters most: <strong>if the gate fails, say so plainly and stop — do not adjust the criteria, and do not soften the report.</strong> A failure means the diagnosis the whole restructure rests on was wrong and it bought only shorter chapters. Better to learn that after one arc than after a book.</p>
</div>

<p>I've read both openings side by side, and I agree — and it's not close. Let me say what I see, because the diagnosis matters for what to do with 116k words.</p>
<p><strong>What the original does in its first 200 words:</strong> Edwin, a ledger, <em>&quot;Pellings, 12:30, soft&quot;</em> — the premise is on the page as an act before anyone explains it. Somebody climbs a hill that makes people reconsider; she doesn't. He sees the sword. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<p><strong>What the rewrite does in its first 900 words:</strong> Peri walks up a hill. Boots, a handkerchief, a smith, orchards she can't read, a speech rehearsed, the Weave's four measures recounted as backstory, a yard, a knock. Nobody speaks. Then a scene break and a <em>second</em> cold open on Edwin. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> The premise doesn't arrive until page four, and the run's report cited <em>that</em> as a virtue (&quot;without a synopsis anywhere near it&quot;).</p>
<p><strong>What I think happened, structurally.</strong> Voice E was synthesised toward Herriot and McPhee texture — prose at rest, the countryside seen closely — and the drafters followed the rules correctly and lost the thing the original had, which is Pratchett's <em>engine</em>: something is happening, and it's funny because of what it is. The original's opening line is a man arranging rain in a ledger. The rewrite's is a hill. And I over-weighted the &quot;openings are entrances, not minutes&quot; instrument until it produced entrances with nothing entering.</p>
<p><strong>The bigger problem is the judging.</strong> Every cold read in the run — four separate Opus agents, reading blind — called this the best opening the project has produced. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> That's a judge failure, not just a drafter one, and it means I can't honestly tell you how much of the 54 chapters shares this fault without you reading more or me finding a judge that agrees with you on <em>this</em> pair first.</p>
<p>Before I do anything: is it the <strong>voice</strong> you're rejecting (Voice E across the board — the close, quiet, notice-the-orchard register), or <strong>this chapter's construction</strong> (the Peri-POV cold open, the delayed premise, the double start)? My own read is the voice, with the construction as its symptom — but you're the one who can see the difference between &quot;worse&quot; and &quot;unreadable,&quot; and the answer decides whether this is a re-voicing of the book or a rethink of the spec.</p>
</div>

The engine of the original scene is <em>comic disagreement</em>; the rewrite's is <em>exposition, politely received</em>.</p>
<p><strong>The opening is a rules casualty too.</strong> &quot;Openings are entrances, not minutes&quot; produced 900 words of entrance. The original's first sentence is a man arranging rain in a ledger — that's an entrance <em>and</em> the premise. I optimised the instrument and lost the event.</p>

<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>

<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<blockquote>

Second bend, and he could see the sword.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The nine-word letter is new — a caller with a stake, in their own units, and a small joke about the Pellings. The original got to the sword in one sentence; I spent three, which is the slowing.</p>

<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br />

<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>

<p>✅ 39 lines, 2116 chars — ## draft/book-01/scenes/02-a-war-horse-on-a-milk-cart.md /   moved     L15-&gt;17  "a second weather," /   moved     L47-&gt;49  "as if he'd ruled something out."</p>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Fixed, tested, and committed (<code>51f5e10</code>); server restarted.</p>
<p><strong>The bug:</strong> the stored context was only consulted when the quote occurred more than once, and then only as an exact 48-character match. A short quote with a single hit anywhere else was accepted as &quot;moved&quot; with no context check; multiple hits with any nearby edit fell through to &quot;nearest to the old offset&quot;. Fuzzy matches ignored context entirely.</p>
<p><strong>The fix:</strong> every non-exact step now scores candidates by how much stored context survives around them — counted character by character outward from the quote, so an edit right beside the selection costs a lot and one far away costs little — and orphans rather than guesses when the best candidate keeps too little. Distance from the old offset only breaks ties. Context stored per new comment is 160 characters each side. Legacy anchors with no context at all resolve only on a unique hit of a quote ≥12 characters.</p>
<p><strong>Regression tests</strong> in <code>tools/commenter/test_relocate.py</code> (7 cases: deleted short quote must orphan, not jump; moved short quote found by context past a lookalike; lightly edited context still picks the right occurrence; legacy long quotes; exact; context width). All pass; two failed before the fix.</p>
<p><strong>On your real comments:</strong> the twelve you left on scene 2 whose lines I rewrote now show as <code>orphaned</code> (correct — the text is gone), the three on surviving lines resolve as <code>moved</code> to the right places, and the groundwork's comments are all still <code>exact</code>. Before the fix, &quot;You&quot; and &quot;clever&quot; were landing on other Yous and clevers.</p>
<p>Also in that commit: the lens's one new finding, <em>&quot;the year he turned ninety&quot;</em> → <em>&quot;two hundred&quot;</em>, in scene 1.</p>
</div>

<p><strong>The bug:</strong> the browser sent only offsets; the server sliced the file <em>on disk</em> at them. Since I edit files while you read, every comment posted after an edit landed on whatever text now sat at the old offset. Now the client sends the selected text plus 160 characters of context, the server anchors on that and relocates it in the current file (or orphans it, never guessing), the response carries the current text so highlights don't drift, and the page re-fetches the file when the tab regains focus. Two new regression tests cover the stale-browser case; 9/9 pass.</p>
<p><strong>Your four scene-3 comments,</strong> all re-anchored to where they were meant and actioned:</p>
<ul>
<li><em>&quot;What is more important than—&quot;</em> → <em>&quot;It isn't an argument. It's a—&quot;</em> — him correcting her framing, cut off, which is his construction.</li>
<li>the frightened-decisions qualification → <em>&quot;He checked, on the way up, whether he was frightened. He was not. He was angry.&quot;</em></li>
<li>the cooldown line → cut, as you said.</li>
<li>the pamphlet → gone from the prose <em>and</em> from canon: the ruling that the church prints a lay pamphlet for unexpected holders is amended in <code>magic.md</code> with your objection quoted; the restrictions now live in the church's ordinary literature on fate magic, which she went and read because she had the class.</li>
</ul>
<p>Scene 3 stands at round 3 in the commenter.</p>
</div>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Fixed and committed (<code>f2b2bd1</code>). <strong>Reload the page</strong> — it's client-side, no server restart needed.</p>
<p><strong>What was wrong.</strong> The function that turns a browser selection into source offsets mishandled the two most common end points a drag produces:</p>
<ul>
<li>an <strong>element boundary</strong> (&quot;after child N&quot;) was mapped to the end of child N — the run of text <em>after</em> the boundary — instead of the end of child N−1. On a paragraph with an italic in it, dragging to just past the italic stored the rest of the paragraph. The old code fails the test <code>&quot;rave&quot;</code> → it returned <code>&quot;rave world.&quot;</code>.</li>
<li>a drag that runs off the end of a line puts its end at offset 0 of the <strong>next paragraph</strong>; the range kept the paragraph break and nothing trimmed it.</li>
</ul>
<p>The popup quote came from the same numbers, so it could look plausible and still highlight more once submitted.</p>
<p><strong>Now:</strong> start points map to the start of the child after the boundary, end points to the end of the child before it, recursively through highlight wrappers and italics; leading/trailing whitespace including paragraph breaks is trimmed off every range. Five cases in <code>tools/commenter/test_selection.js</code> (run with <code>node</code>), all passing; the README lists both test files.</p>
<p>That's three commenter bugs today, all of which were mangling <em>your</em> feedback specifically. If you see a fourth, say so early — I'd rather fix the instrument than guess at what a comment meant.</p>
</div>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>The skill is written and committed (<code>631a99d</code>): <strong><code>.claude/skills/pre-david-pass/SKILL.md</code></strong>, now the editing subagent's brief, run once per scene before you first see it, alongside the canon lens.</p>
<p>What I found sorting 69 comments: they fall into seven questions, and the order is by how often you ask them.</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Does this sentence actually make sense?</strong> — by far the largest group (~25). The skill's test is the flat restatement, with your examples for each way a sentence fails it: no restatement, two restatements, a word doing a job it can't, an antecedent-less reference, a reply that doesn't answer the line before, a missing transition, a count or fact that's false inside the scene, an inference that doesn't follow.</li>
<li><strong>The gesture at meaning</strong> — &quot;cheap and annoying&quot;, &quot;doesn't cash out&quot;, &quot;weird comparison&quot;, the announcing fragment, the footnoted joke.</li>
<li><strong>Would this exist in a real world occupied by real people?</strong> — literacy, ages, institutional chronology, what people would <em>actually</em> say, rare things staying rare; and your rule on inventing, verbatim.</li>
<li><strong>Why would this character do or say this?</strong> — nobody says a thing because the reader needs it; knowledge states; beliefs with histories; a character being wrong at the right resolution.</li>
<li><strong>Is the device working, or only the sentence?</strong> — a repeated comment is about the device; never shuffle words; asserted-not-shown; recitation that should be conversation; threads raised and dropped; the line you liked versus the line you queried.</li>
<li><strong>Weak where it should be strong</strong> — the smallest group, and you supply the fix every time: plainest form, &quot;did not&quot; over &quot;had not&quot;, idiom over coinage.</li>
<li><strong>Scenes as wholes</strong> — engines not entrances, Edwin first, Peri's wrong theory, 1.4k is fine, notes go where the thing is worked, retaining the original versus reflecting a ruling.</li>
</ol>
<p>Every check carries your words for what failing looks like, so the subagent is matching against you rather than against a rule. It also carries the standing instruction not to touch anything that isn't a finding.</p>
<p>If you want to read it before it's used, now's the time; otherwise I'll start scene 4 — four minutes late, and the trowels — with this pass on it before you see it.</p>
</div>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>World-building question: How does magic work? <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<p>I suggest as a starting point, run a dynamic workflow with haiku agents over all the notes and various iterations of the story to look for information about this and gather it all up. Treat old versions of the story as non-canon but suggestive. Then try to synthesize this information. Recommend reading up Brandon Sanderson's theories about magic systems, but his tend to be a bit mechanical and I don't mind having this as a slightly more mystical thing.</p>
<p>It doesn't need to be anything particularly innovative in magic system design, I just want answers.</p>
<p>As part of this I would like an answer to what fate magic does and how it works, and what different types of magic are out there and their mechanism of action (e.g. we should also rule on healing magic. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<p>Something that I'm imagining as back story (should never appear in the plot as a major feature) is that the system is actually a magical working created by a long-fallen civilisation (so long ago that you barely see their remnants without doing significant archaeology. That's why the system date is so high), built up over time as mana levels in the world increased to cataclysmic levels, and we are seeing the civilisation that emerged in the last couple thousand years since mana levels have receded somewhat and the system managed to build more dungeons to contain more of it. (This is all very route 1 litrpg, but that's fine, it's not an area we are trying to innovate in). This means that magic should be consistent with being able to build something like the system, but with current magical knowledge falling very far short of how to do that.</p></blockquote>

<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted</span>, the keeping charm's push, the growth-hold, the Seal. <li><strong>(C) Both</strong> — an unseen container distinct from the Deeps; harmless and pointless.</li>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>I think A is the right call. The System is designed as a mana sink, and the place it puts mana is people. How about this: Deeps and other dungeons and monster emergences are an emergent phenomena of mana, not a property of the system, but the system is designed in large part to contain and drain them. Does that fit?</p>
<p>Side note: I think there should be system notifications on entering a deep.</p></blockquote>


<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Mostly, with one reframing, and the notification costs one sentence in a sealed file.</p>
<p><strong>What fits as-is:</strong> the System as a mana sink whose vessel is people — that's (A) exactly, and it's now the sealed backstory.</p>
<p><strong>What collides:</strong> &quot;Deeps are an emergent phenomenon of mana.&quot; The sealed Deep answer commits, as its foundation, to the <em>holes</em> having no magical component at all — the adversarial critiques forced that, and the Frostmonth and <span class="redacted">redacted</span> beats are built on it. It also commits to the System having nothing to do with Deeps: no authorship, no notice, no telemetry.</p>
<p><strong>The reframing I'd propose, which I think is your idea with the noun moved:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>The holes are natural. <strong>The life in them is what mana does to life in the dark</strong> — &quot;put together on a different principle&quot; because that's what a body becoming its own idea of itself does over ten thousand years with no sun and a lot of give. The sealed answer deliberately left the fauna's origin unanswered (&quot;not load-bearing&quot;), so this slots in without touching anything. Monsters generally — dungeon fauna, the wild margins, the sea — are mana-emergent life; breaks and leakage stay ecological, as written.</li>
<li><strong>The System drains them by making delving the best levelling there is.</strong> That's already your canon (delving levels a class unusually well; dungeons supply achievements). Under the backstory it's the working harvesting give out of monster-life into people, which is what it was built to do. And it <em>contains</em> them the way it contains everything: less loose give in the world, fewer things being otherwise unasked. Neither requires the System to know a hole exists — it only counts what a person did, which it does anyway.</li>
</ul>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Something flat, on crossing the chain — <code>[Holm Deep.]</code> or <code>[Delving: Holm Deep.]</code> — and never a word about condition, depth, danger, or what's in it. Cost: one sentence in <code>deeps.md</code> §11 and a line in its clue ledger, plus a note that the <em>absence</em> of a box is itself a thing wardens use (a mouth the System doesn't name is a hole nobody has delved to the bottom of, and the drafting rule is that the System names only mouths with a delve record behind them — which keeps the notification a fact about people, not about holes).</p>
<p>Two lines from you and I'll write both in: (1) yes to the reframing, and (2) the box's wording and whether it fires for everyone who crosses the chain or only for someone going down.</p>
</div>


<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>Yes agree that the notification should not overlap with <span class="redacted">redacted</span>'s skill.</p>
<p>Suggestion: The system does not track anything about the deep, but it does track your stats on delves. When you delve, you get something like a kill count, a &quot;total value&quot; count which tracks something to do with the total amount you're clearing, tracking something like the total mana you've drained from the deep. On exit you get a notification congratulating you on your delve, with some rating of it, granting achievements for personal-bests, and occasionally granting awards (stat boosts, skill boosts for skills you used in the dungeon - effectively lowering the threshold). Maybe there's a system screen where you can see your whole history of past delves. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p></blockquote>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>I like this very much.</p>
<p>A few notes: I think &quot;farm magic&quot; starts at the 5 cantrips, it doesn't stop at it. e.g. the revealings are clearly farm magic, and are not part of the cantrip.</p>

I applied nearly all of it without judgement because I was running an assembly line — sixty-five scenes in a day, four lens reports at a time. <li>The engines in <code>SCENES.md</code> are summaries of the <em>old</em> chapters, so even the plan was pulling me back toward them.</li>

<li><strong>A rule for dialogue in the memo, before I write a line:</strong> every exchange must be understandable to someone who has not read the previous scene; people say the thing, and the wit is in what happens, not in what is withheld. Callback phrases don't get to carry meaning on their own.</li>
